People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, Dr. [Name]. Thank you for seeing me. So, honestly, what really made me finally pick up the phone and make this appointment—and I've been debating it for a few months now, probably ever since we shifted to this new quarterly planning model at my agency—is that I keep getting up during our long sales strategy meetings. 

I'm an advertising sales agent, and we have these massive, two- or three-hour meetings where we're going over ad buys and client pitches, and I know I’m supposed to just stay seated and take notes like everyone else. But I just... I can't seem to stay in my chair. I'll constantly find reasons to get up. I'll get up just to refill my water, even if my glass is practically full and I really don't need a drink, or I'll wander over and pace by the printer in the back of the conference room, acting like I'm waiting for a slide deck to print out when I'm really not. 

And the thing is, I know how it looks. The room is mostly glass, everyone else is perfectly planted in their ergonomic chairs, and I'm just popping up and down. My boss even gave me this look last Tuesday when I got up for the third time during the VP's presentation. It's just so frustrating because I know the expectation is to remain seated, but I just end up making excuses to pace around the back of the room anyway, and I feel like it's starting to become a whole thing at work.

You know, it's funny you ask that, because I was actually just thinking about this the other day. I don't really have that problem at all outside of work. Like, if I'm at home? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's definitely the concentration thing. I mean, I don't feel anxious or stressed about the meetings themselves, really. I know my accounts, I know the clients, so it's not like I'm sitting there terrified they're going to call on me and I won't know the answer. It's more like... okay, so we have these really long strategy meetings, right? And they always put up these massive PowerPoint decks. They're just so dense, with all these charts and projections. 

I'll sit down, and I'll have my notebook open, and I'll tell myself, 'Okay, this is the Q3 planning meeting, you need to pay attention, this is important.' And I really do start out focused. I'll listen to the first few minutes, maybe jot down a note about a new ad buy or something. But then... I don't know, it's like my brain just sort of unplugs? It happens when I'm reading client briefs at my desk, too. I'll be reading this three-page brief about a new campaign for a local car dealership, and I'll read the first paragraph, and I'm totally with it. But then, suddenly, five or ten minutes have gone by, and I realize I've just been staring at the exact same slide on the projector, or the exact same paragraph on my screen, and I have absolutely no idea what the last point was. 

It's like I'm physically looking at the words, but they aren't going into my brain. The VP will be talking about, I don't know, demographic targeting in the tri-state area, and I'll hear the sound of his voice, but I'm not actually processing a single thing he's saying. And then I have to frantically try to figure out where we are in the deck, or I have to reread the same paragraph in the brief like three or four times just to make it stick. That's usually when I start feeling like I need to get up and walk around, because I'm just sitting there, totally checked out, realizing I've completely lost the thread of the meeting again. It's so frustrating because I really am trying to pay attention, I just... I can't seem to hold onto it for more than a few minutes before I just drift off into space.

So, honestly, what really made me finally pick up the phone and make this appointment—and I've been debating it for a few months now, probably ever since we shifted to this new quarterly planning model at my agency—is that I keep getting up during our long sales strategy meetings. \n\nI'm an advertising sales agent, and we have these massive, two- or three-hour meetings where we're going over ad buys and client pitches, and I know I’m supposed to just stay seated and take notes like everyone else. But I just... I can't seem to stay in my chair. I'll constantly find reasons to get up. I'll get up just to refill my water, even if my glass is practically full and I really don't need a drink, or I'll wander over and pace by the printer in the back of the conference room, acting like I'm waiting for a slide deck to print out when I'm really not. \n\nAnd the thing is, I know how it looks. The room is mostly glass, everyone else is perfectly planted in their ergonomic chairs, and I'm just popping up and down. My boss even gave me this look last Tuesday when I got up for the third time during the VP's presentation. It's just so frustrating because I know the expectation is to remain seated, but I just end up making excuses to pace around the back of the room anyway, and I feel like it's starting to become a whole thing at work.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these work meetings, do you find yourself having similar difficulties sitting still in other situations—like during meals at home, watching TV, or in social gatherings?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because I was actually just thinking about this the other day. I don't really have that problem at all outside of work. Like, if I'm at home? I can sit there for literally hours. Just this past Sunday, my boyfriend and I binge-watched almost an entire season of this new true-crime documentary on Netflix, and I was just totally glued to the sofa. I had my blanket, my snacks, and I didn't even want to get up to go to the kitchen. I just sat there the whole afternoon, completely fine. \n\nAnd with meals, it's the same thing. We usually eat on the couch, honestly, but even when we do sit at the actual dining table—like he made this really elaborate baked ziti a couple of nights ago, so we sat at the table to make it a nice dinner—I didn't feel that antsy, crawling-out-of-my-skin feeling at all. We just sat and ate and talked for probably an hour, and I didn't once feel the urge to jump up and check the mail or anything. \n\nEven in social situations, like if I'm out with my girlfriends... we have this spot downtown where we always get a corner booth, and we'll be there for three hours just catching up and having drinks, and I'm perfectly happy just staying put in the booth. I never feel like I need to go pace by the bathrooms or make up an excuse to stand. \n\nSo, no, I really haven't noticed it outside of the office. It's so weird, right? It really just seems to be those specific, long, quiet conference room situations where I'm supposed to be a captive audience and look professional. When it's my own time, or I'm just relaxing or hanging out, I don't have that problem at all. I can just chill. It's just... I don't know, maybe it's the fluorescent lights at the agency or the pressure of the VP being right there, but yeah, at home or out and about, I'm totally fine sitting still.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting in these long meetings and feeling the urge to get up, are you finding it hard to concentrate on what's being said, or is it more that you're feeling anxious or stressed about the meeting situation itself?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely the concentration thing. I mean, I don't feel anxious or stressed about the meetings themselves, really. I know my accounts, I know the clients, so it's not like I'm sitting there terrified they're going to call on me and I won't know the answer. It's more like... okay, so we have these really long strategy meetings, right? And they always put up these massive PowerPoint decks. They're just so dense, with all these charts and projections. \n\nI'll sit down, and I'll have my notebook open, and I'll tell myself, 'Okay, this is the Q3 planning meeting, you need to pay attention, this is important.' And I really do start out focused. I'll listen to the first few minutes, maybe jot down a note about a new ad buy or something. But then... I don't know, it's like my brain just sort of unplugs? It happens when I'm reading client briefs at my desk, too. I'll be reading this three-page brief about a new campaign for a local car dealership, and I'll read the first paragraph, and I'm totally with it. But then, suddenly, five or ten minutes have gone by, and I realize I've just been staring at the exact same slide on the projector, or the exact same paragraph on my screen, and I have absolutely no idea what the last point was. \n\nIt's like I'm physically looking at the words, but they aren't going into my brain. The VP will be talking about, I don't know, demographic targeting in the tri-state area, and I'll hear the sound of his voice, but I'm not actually processing a single thing he's saying. And then I have to frantically try to figure out where we are in the deck, or I have to reread the same paragraph in the brief like three or four times just to make it stick. That's usually when I start feeling like I need to get up and walk around, because I'm just sitting there, totally checked out, realizing I've completely lost the thread of the meeting again. It's so frustrating because I really am trying to pay attention, I just... I can't seem to hold onto it for more than a few minutes before I just drift off into space.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. When it's my own time, or I'm just relaxing or hanging out, I don't have that problem at all. I can just chill. It's just... I don't know, maybe it's the fluorescent lights at the agency or the pressure of the VP being right there, but yeah, at home or out and about, I'm totally fine sitting still.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting in these long meetings and feeling the urge to get up, are you finding it hard to concentrate on what's being said, or is it more that you're feeling anxious or stressed about the meeting situation itself?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely the concentration thing. I mean, I don't feel anxious or stressed about the meetings themselves, really. I know my accounts, I know the clients, so it's not like I'm sitting there terrified they're going to call on me and I won't know the answer. It's more like... okay, so we have these really long strategy meetings, right? And they always put up these massive PowerPoint decks. They're just so dense, with all these charts and projections. \n\nI'll sit down, and I'll have my notebook open, and I'll tell myself, 'Okay, this is the Q3 planning meeting, you need to pay attention, this is important.' And I really do start out focused. I'll listen to the first few minutes, maybe jot down a note about a new ad buy or something. But then... I don't know, it's like my brain just sort of unplugs? It happens when I'm reading client briefs at my desk, too. I'll be reading this three-page brief about a new campaign for a local car dealership, and I'll read the first paragraph, and I'm totally with it. But then, suddenly, five or ten minutes have gone by, and I realize I've just been staring at the exact same slide on the projector, or the exact same paragraph on my screen, and I have absolutely no idea what the last point was. \n\nIt's like I'm physically looking at the words, but they aren't going into my brain. The VP will be talking about, I don't know, demographic targeting in the tri-state area, and I'll hear the sound of his voice, but I'm not actually processing a single thing he's saying. And then I have to frantically try to figure out where we are in the deck, or I have to reread the same paragraph in the brief like three or four times just to make it stick. That's usually when I start feeling like I need to get up and walk around, because I'm just sitting there, totally checked out, realizing I've completely lost the thread of the meeting again. It's so frustrating because I really am trying to pay attention, I just... I can't seem to hold onto it for more than a few minutes before I just drift off into space.\n\n[Last Question]\nGoing back to when you were in school—elementary, middle, high school—did you have similar difficulties concentrating or staying focused on lessons and assignments back then, or is this something that's more of a recent problem that started around the time of the new quarterly planning model at work?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 30 / advertising_sales_agent / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"I remember this starting way before high school—my elementary school teachers wrote that I was bright but rushed, talked out of turn, forgot homework, and had a desk stuffed with half-finished papers.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, gosh, no, it's definitely not just a recent thing with the new planning model. I mean, the planning model just brought it out in a way that affects my job, but if I really think back... I remember this starting way, way before high school. Like, elementary school. 

My mom actually kept all my old report cards in this big plastic bin in the attic, and we were looking through them a couple of years ago when she moved, and it was honestly kind of eye-opening. Because every single teacher, from like second grade all the way up, wrote the exact same things in the comments section. They would always say I was a really bright kid, which, you know, is nice, but then there was always a catch. My elementary school teachers wrote that I was bright but I rushed through my work, or that I talked out of turn constantly. I was always interrupting people or just blurting things out because I felt like if I didn't say it right that second, my thought would just completely vanish. 

And the homework... oh my gosh, the homework. I would forget it all the time. I'd either forget to write down what the assignment was, or I'd actually do it at home and then just completely forget to turn it in the next morning. My desk was an absolute disaster zone, too. I remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she actually made me stay in during recess once just to clean it out, because it was literally stuffed to the brim with all these crumpled, half-finished papers and old worksheets that I just shoved in there instead of putting them away in my folders. 

So, yeah, it is definitely not new. It is like I have always been this way, just sort of scattered and leaving a trail of unfinished stuff, and I guess I just figured out how to handle it better or hide it better when I got to college and started working, right up until these massive three-hour agency meetings started and I couldn't just skate by on my own schedule anymore...

Just this past Sunday, my boyfriend and I binge-watched almost an entire season of this new true-crime documentary on Netflix, and I was just totally glued to the sofa. I had my blanket, my snacks, and I didn't even want to get up to go to the kitchen. I just sat there the whole afternoon, completely fine. \n\nAnd with meals, it's the same thing. We usually eat on the couch, honestly, but even when we do sit at the actual dining table—like he made this really elaborate baked ziti a couple of nights ago, so we sat at the table to make it a nice dinner—I didn't feel that antsy, crawling-out-of-my-skin feeling at all. We just sat and ate and talked for probably an hour, and I didn't once feel the urge to jump up and check the mail or anything. \n\nEven in social situations, like if I'm out with my girlfriends... we have this spot downtown where we always get a corner booth, and we'll be there for three hours just catching up and having drinks, and I'm perfectly happy just staying put in the booth. I never feel like I need to go pace by the bathrooms or make up an excuse to stand. \n\nSo, no, I really haven't noticed it outside of the office. It's so weird, right? It really just seems to be those specific, long, quiet conference room situations where I'm supposed to be a captive audience and look professional. When it's my own time, or I'm just relaxing or hanging out, I don't have that problem at all. I can just chill. It's just... I don't know, maybe it's the fluorescent lights at the agency or the pressure of the VP being right there, but yeah, at home or out and about, I'm totally fine sitting still.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting in these long meetings and feeling the urge to get up, are you finding it hard to concentrate on what's being said, or is it more that you're feeling anxious or stressed about the meeting situation itself?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely the concentration thing. I mean, I don't feel anxious or stressed about the meetings themselves, really. I know my accounts, I know the clients, so it's not like I'm sitting there terrified they're going to call on me and I won't know the answer. It's more like... okay, so we have these really long strategy meetings, right? And they always put up these massive PowerPoint decks. They're just so dense, with all these charts and projections. \n\nI'll sit down, and I'll have my notebook open, and I'll tell myself, 'Okay, this is the Q3 planning meeting, you need to pay attention, this is important.' And I really do start out focused. I'll listen to the first few minutes, maybe jot down a note about a new ad buy or something. But then... I don't know, it's like my brain just sort of unplugs? It happens when I'm reading client briefs at my desk, too. I'll be reading this three-page brief about a new campaign for a local car dealership, and I'll read the first paragraph, and I'm totally with it. But then, suddenly, five or ten minutes have gone by, and I realize I've just been staring at the exact same slide on the projector, or the exact same paragraph on my screen, and I have absolutely no idea what the last point was. \n\nIt's like I'm physically looking at the words, but they aren't going into my brain. The VP will be talking about, I don't know, demographic targeting in the tri-state area, and I'll hear the sound of his voice, but I'm not actually processing a single thing he's saying. And then I have to frantically try to figure out where we are in the deck, or I have to reread the same paragraph in the brief like three or four times just to make it stick. That's usually when I start feeling like I need to get up and walk around, because I'm just sitting there, totally checked out, realizing I've completely lost the thread of the meeting again. It's so frustrating because I really am trying to pay attention, I just... I can't seem to hold onto it for more than a few minutes before I just drift off into space.\n\nDoctor: Going back to when you were in school—elementary, middle, high school—did you have similar difficulties concentrating or staying focused on lessons and assignments back then, or is this something that's more of a recent problem that started around the time of the new quarterly planning model at work?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no, it's definitely not just a recent thing with the new planning model. I mean, the planning model just brought it out in a way that affects my job, but if I really think back... I remember this starting way, way before high school. Like, elementary school. \n\nMy mom actually kept all my old report cards in this big plastic bin in the attic, and we were looking through them a couple of years ago when she moved, and it was honestly kind of eye-opening. Because every single teacher, from like second grade all the way up, wrote the exact same things in the comments section. They would always say I was a really bright kid, which, you know, is nice, but then there was always a catch. My elementary school teachers wrote that I was bright but I rushed through my work, or that I talked out of turn constantly. I was always interrupting people or just blurting things out because I felt like if I didn't say it right that second, my thought would just completely vanish. \n\nAnd the homework... oh my gosh, the homework. I would forget it all the time. I'd either forget to write down what the assignment was, or I'd actually do it at home and then just completely forget to turn it in the next morning. My desk was an absolute disaster zone, too. I remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she actually made me stay in during recess once just to clean it out, because it was literally stuffed to the brim with all these crumpled, half-finished papers and old worksheets that I just shoved in there instead of putting them away in my folders. \n\nSo, yeah, it is definitely not new. It is like I have always been this way, just sort of scattered and leaving a trail of unfinished stuff, and I guess I just figured out how to handle it better or hide it better when I got to college and started working, right up until these massive three-hour agency meetings started and I couldn't just skate by on my own schedule anymore...\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you were younger and had those thoughts that felt like they would disappear if you didn't say them right away—the ones where you'd blurt things out in class—do you still experience that same feeling now as an adult, where you feel like you have to act on something immediately or the urge will go away?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, um... no, actually. I don't think I really do that anymore. I mean, I think I kind of grew out of that specific thing? Or maybe adult life is just set up differently, you know? Like, back in elementary school, you're sitting there at your little desk and the teacher is at the chalkboard, and if you don't raise your hand right that exact second, the moment is completely gone and the class moves on to math or whatever. But now, working at the agency... well, first of all, if I just blurted things out during a pitch with a major client, my VP would probably have a literal heart attack right there in the conference room. So I definitely learned not to do that! 

And I don't really get that panicked, bubbling-up feeling of, like, 'I have to say this right this second or it's gone forever' anymore. If I have an idea for a campaign, or a question about a media buy while someone is talking, I just... I don't know, I'll jot it down on a sticky note. My desk is literally covered in those little neon pink and yellow Post-its for that exact reason. Or, honestly, I'll just shoot my coworker a Slack message. Slack is like a lifesaver for that, right? Because you can just type the thought the very second it pops into your head without actually interrupting anyone's sentence or derailing the whole meeting. 

So, yeah, I haven't really noticed that being a problem now. I mean, I talk a lot, obviously—my boyfriend always jokes that I could talk to a brick wall and get it to buy ad space—but I don't get that impulsive, desperate urge to just spit words out before they vanish like I did when I was a kid. I feel like I have a pretty good filter now, or at least I just know how to hold onto a thought, or write it down, until it's actually my turn to speak.

So, honestly, what really made me finally pick up the phone and make this appointment—and I've been debating it for a few months now, probably ever since we shifted to this new quarterly planning model at my agency—is that I keep getting up during our long sales strategy meetings. \n\nI'm an advertising sales agent, and we have these massive, two- or three-hour meetings where we're going over ad buys and client pitches, and I know I’m supposed to just stay seated and take notes like everyone else. But I just... I can't seem to stay in my chair. I'll constantly find reasons to get up. I'll get up just to refill my water, even if my glass is practically full and I really don't need a drink, or I'll wander over and pace by the printer in the back of the conference room, acting like I'm waiting for a slide deck to print out when I'm really not. \n\nAnd the thing is, I know how it looks. The room is mostly glass, everyone else is perfectly planted in their ergonomic chairs, and I'm just popping up and down. My boss even gave me this look last Tuesday when I got up for the third time during the VP's presentation. It's just so frustrating because I know the expectation is to remain seated, but I just end up making excuses to pace around the back of the room anyway, and I feel like it's starting to become a whole thing at work.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these work meetings, do you find yourself having similar difficulties sitting still in other situations—like during meals at home, watching TV, or in social gatherings?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because I was actually just thinking about this the other day. I don't really have that problem at all outside of work. Like, if I'm at home? I can sit there for literally hours. Just this past Sunday, my boyfriend and I binge-watched almost an entire season of this new true-crime documentary on Netflix, and I was just totally glued to the sofa. I had my blanket, my snacks, and I didn't even want to get up to go to the kitchen. I just sat there the whole afternoon, completely fine. \n\nAnd with meals, it's the same thing. We usually eat on the couch, honestly, but even when we do sit at the actual dining table—like he made this really elaborate baked ziti a couple of nights ago, so we sat at the table to make it a nice dinner—I didn't feel that antsy, crawling-out-of-my-skin feeling at all. We just sat and ate and talked for probably an hour, and I didn't once feel the urge to jump up and check the mail or anything. \n\nEven in social situations, like if I'm out with my girlfriends... we have this spot downtown where we always get a corner booth, and we'll be there for three hours just catching up and having drinks, and I'm perfectly happy just staying put in the booth. I never feel like I need to go pace by the bathrooms or make up an excuse to stand. \n\nSo, no, I really haven't noticed it outside of the office. It's so weird, right? It really just seems to be those specific, long, quiet conference room situations where I'm supposed to be a captive audience and look professional. When it's my own time, or I'm just relaxing or hanging out, I don't have that problem at all. I can just chill. It's just... I don't know, maybe it's the fluorescent lights at the agency or the pressure of the VP being right there, but yeah, at home or out and about, I'm totally fine sitting still.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting in these long meetings and feeling the urge to get up, are you finding it hard to concentrate on what's being said, or is it more that you're feeling anxious or stressed about the meeting situation itself?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely the concentration thing. I mean, I don't feel anxious or stressed about the meetings themselves, really. I know my accounts, I know the clients, so it's not like I'm sitting there terrified they're going to call on me and I won't know the answer. It's more like... okay, so we have these really long strategy meetings, right? And they always put up these massive PowerPoint decks. They're just so dense, with all these charts and projections. \n\nI'll sit down, and I'll have my notebook open, and I'll tell myself, 'Okay, this is the Q3 planning meeting, you need to pay attention, this is important.' And I really do start out focused. I'll listen to the first few minutes, maybe jot down a note about a new ad buy or something. But then... I don't know, it's like my brain just sort of unplugs? It happens when I'm reading client briefs at my desk, too. I'll be reading this three-page brief about a new campaign for a local car dealership, and I'll read the first paragraph, and I'm totally with it. But then, suddenly, five or ten minutes have gone by, and I realize I've just been staring at the exact same slide on the projector, or the exact same paragraph on my screen, and I have absolutely no idea what the last point was. \n\nIt's like I'm physically looking at the words, but they aren't going into my brain. The VP will be talking about, I don't know, demographic targeting in the tri-state area, and I'll hear the sound of his voice, but I'm not actually processing a single thing he's saying. And then I have to frantically try to figure out where we are in the deck, or I have to reread the same paragraph in the brief like three or four times just to make it stick. That's usually when I start feeling like I need to get up and walk around, because I'm just sitting there, totally checked out, realizing I've completely lost the thread of the meeting again. It's so frustrating because I really am trying to pay attention, I just... I can't seem to hold onto it for more than a few minutes before I just drift off into space.\n\nDoctor: Going back to when you were in school—elementary, middle, high school—did you have similar difficulties concentrating or staying focused on lessons and assignments back then, or is this something that's more of a recent problem that started around the time of the new quarterly planning model at work?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no, it's definitely not just a recent thing with the new planning model. I mean, the planning model just brought it out in a way that affects my job, but if I really think back... I remember this starting way, way before high school. Like, elementary school. \n\nMy mom actually kept all my old report cards in this big plastic bin in the attic, and we were looking through them a couple of years ago when she moved, and it was honestly kind of eye-opening. Because every single teacher, from like second grade all the way up, wrote the exact same things in the comments section. They would always say I was a really bright kid, which, you know, is nice, but then there was always a catch. My elementary school teachers wrote that I was bright but I rushed through my work, or that I talked out of turn constantly. I was always interrupting people or just blurting things out because I felt like if I didn't say it right that second, my thought would just completely vanish. \n\nAnd the homework... oh my gosh, the homework. I would forget it all the time. I'd either forget to write down what the assignment was, or I'd actually do it at home and then just completely forget to turn it in the next morning. My desk was an absolute disaster zone, too. I remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she actually made me stay in during recess once just to clean it out, because it was literally stuffed to the brim with all these crumpled, half-finished papers and old worksheets that I just shoved in there instead of putting them away in my folders. \n\nSo, yeah, it is definitely not new. It is like I have always been this way, just sort of scattered and leaving a trail of unfinished stuff, and I guess I just figured out how to handle it better or hide it better when I got to college and started working, right up until these massive three-hour agency meetings started and I couldn't just skate by on my own schedule anymore...\n\nDoctor: When you were younger and had those thoughts that felt like they would disappear if you didn't say them right away—the ones where you'd blurt things out in class—do you still experience that same feeling now as an adult, where you feel like you have to act on something immediately or the urge will go away?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, actually. I don't think I really do that anymore. I mean, I think I kind of grew out of that specific thing? Or maybe adult life is just set up differently, you know? Like, back in elementary school, you're sitting there at your little desk and the teacher is at the chalkboard, and if you don't raise your hand right that exact second, the moment is completely gone and the class moves on to math or whatever. But now, working at the agency... well, first of all, if I just blurted things out during a pitch with a major client, my VP would probably have a literal heart attack right there in the conference room. So I definitely learned not to do that! \n\nAnd I don't really get that panicked, bubbling-up feeling of, like, 'I have to say this right this second or it's gone forever' anymore. If I have an idea for a campaign, or a question about a media buy while someone is talking, I just... I don't know, I'll jot it down on a sticky note. My desk is literally covered in those little neon pink and yellow Post-its for that exact reason. Or, honestly, I'll just shoot my coworker a Slack message. Slack is like a lifesaver for that, right? Because you can just type the thought the very second it pops into your head without actually interrupting anyone's sentence or derailing the whole meeting. \n\nSo, yeah, I haven't really noticed that being a problem now. I mean, I talk a lot, obviously—my boyfriend always jokes that I could talk to a brick wall and get it to buy ad space—but I don't get that impulsive, desperate urge to just spit words out before they vanish like I did when I was a kid. I feel like I have a pretty good filter now, or at least I just know how to hold onto a thought, or write it down, until it's actually my turn to speak.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, the Post-its... well, I mean, they work for getting the thought out of my head in that exact second, like I was saying, but as an actual organizational system? It's... it's kind of a disaster, honestly. I try so hard to be organized, I really do. Every January, I go out and buy one of those really expensive, fancy leather-bound planners—you know the ones, where they have the hourly breakdowns and the little inspirational quotes at the top of every page, and the different colored ribbons to mark your place? I always tell myself, 'Okay, this is the year I'm going to have it all together, everything goes in the planner.' 

But then reality hits, and things get busy. I'll be on a call with a client—like last week, I was talking to the marketing director for this regional grocery chain we represent, and they were changing their whole Q4 strategy—and I'll just frantically scribble a note on a pink sticky note because it's right there. But then later, I'll be on my phone on the train and I'll email myself a reminder about their ad spend. And then I'll get to my desk and open a tab in Chrome with their competitor's pricing, and I just leave the tab open... I have like, I don't know, eighty tabs open across three different browser windows right now. 

So when it actually comes time to sit down and build the campaign strategy or put together the pitch deck, my notes are just completely scattered everywhere. They end up split between my emails, the sticky notes plastered all over my monitor, that expensive planner which I only half-use, and all those random browser tabs. It's just a total mess. And because it's so fragmented, I end up wasting just a ridiculous amount of time trying to piece it all back together. I'll sit there at my desk for forty-five minutes or an hour just staring at everything, trying to figure out what I'm supposed to do first, or which note is the most urgent, instead of just starting the actual work. 

It's like I have to go on a scavenger hunt through my own workspace before I can even begin to do my job. My boyfriend is always telling me I should just use Trello or Asana or one of those project management apps, because that's what his tech company uses, but I feel like if I try to learn a new digital system, I'll just end up with notes in Trello and on the sticky notes and in my email, you know? It wouldn't actually fix the problem, it would just add another place for me to lose my thoughts. So, yeah, I definitely wouldn't say I feel organized overall. It's more like controlled chaos, except half the time I'm not even the one controlling it.

  {

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n6\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Dr. [Name]. Thank you for seeing me. So, honestly, what really made me finally pick up the phone and make this appointment—and I've been debating it for a few months now, probably ever since we shifted to this new quarterly planning model at my agency—is that I keep getting up during our long sales strategy meetings. \n\nI'm an advertising sales agent, and we have these massive, two- or three-hour meetings where we're going over ad buys and client pitches, and I know I’m supposed to just stay seated and take notes like everyone else. But I just... I can't seem to stay in my chair. I'll constantly find reasons to get up. I'll get up just to refill my water, even if my glass is practically full and I really don't need a drink, or I'll wander over and pace by the printer in the back of the conference room, acting like I'm waiting for a slide deck to print out when I'm really not. \n\nAnd the thing is, I know how it looks. The room is mostly glass, everyone else is perfectly planted in their ergonomic chairs, and I'm just popping up and down. My boss even gave me this look last Tuesday when I got up for the third time during the VP's presentation. It's just so frustrating because I know the expectation is to remain seated, but I just end up making excuses to pace around the back of the room anyway, and I feel like it's starting to become a whole thing at work.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these work meetings, do you find yourself having similar difficulties sitting still in other situations—like during meals at home, watching TV, or in social gatherings?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because I was actually just thinking about this the other day. I don't really have that problem at all outside of work. Like, if I'm at home? I can sit there for literally hours. Just this past Sunday, my boyfriend and I binge-watched almost an entire season of this new true-crime documentary on Netflix, and I was just totally glued to the sofa. I had my blanket, my snacks, and I didn't even want to get up to go to the kitchen. I just sat there the whole afternoon, completely fine. \n\nAnd with meals, it's the same thing. We usually eat on the couch, honestly, but even when we do sit at the actual dining table—like he made this really elaborate baked ziti a couple of nights ago, so we sat at the table to make it a nice dinner—I didn't feel that antsy, crawling-out-of-my-skin feeling at all. We just sat and ate and talked for probably an hour, and I didn't once feel the urge to jump up and check the mail or anything. \n\nEven in social situations, like if I'm out with my girlfriends... we have this spot downtown where we always get a corner booth, and we'll be there for three hours just catching up and having drinks, and I'm perfectly happy just staying put in the booth. I never feel like I need to go pace by the bathrooms or make up an excuse to stand. \n\nSo, no, I really haven't noticed it outside of the office. It's so weird, right? It really just seems to be those specific, long, quiet conference room situations where I'm supposed to be a captive audience and look professional. When it's my own time, or I'm just relaxing or hanging out, I don't have that problem at all. I can just chill. It's just... I don't know, maybe it's the fluorescent lights at the agency or the pressure of the VP being right there, but yeah, at home or out and about, I'm totally fine sitting still.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting in these long meetings and feeling the urge to get up, are you finding it hard to concentrate on what's being said, or is it more that you're feeling anxious or stressed about the meeting situation itself?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely the concentration thing. I mean, I don't feel anxious or stressed about the meetings themselves, really. I know my accounts, I know the clients, so it's not like I'm sitting there terrified they're going to call on me and I won't know the answer. It's more like... okay, so we have these really long strategy meetings, right? And they always put up these massive PowerPoint decks. They're just so dense, with all these charts and projections. \n\nI'll sit down, and I'll have my notebook open, and I'll tell myself, 'Okay, this is the Q3 planning meeting, you need to pay attention, this is important.' And I really do start out focused. I'll listen to the first few minutes, maybe jot down a note about a new ad buy or something. But then... I don't know, it's like my brain just sort of unplugs? It happens when I'm reading client briefs at my desk, too. I'll be reading this three-page brief about a new campaign for a local car dealership, and I'll read the first paragraph, and I'm totally with it. But then, suddenly, five or ten minutes have gone by, and I realize I've just been staring at the exact same slide on the projector, or the exact same paragraph on my screen, and I have absolutely no idea what the last point was. \n\nIt's like I'm physically looking at the words, but they aren't going into my brain. The VP will be talking about, I don't know, demographic targeting in the tri-state area, and I'll hear the sound of his voice, but I'm not actually processing a single thing he's saying. And then I have to frantically try to figure out where we are in the deck, or I have to reread the same paragraph in the brief like three or four times just to make it stick. That's usually when I start feeling like I need to get up and walk around, because I'm just sitting there, totally checked out, realizing I've completely lost the thread of the meeting again. It's so frustrating because I really am trying to pay attention, I just... I can't seem to hold onto it for more than a few minutes before I just drift off into space.\n\nDoctor: Going back to when you were in school—elementary, middle, high school—did you have similar difficulties concentrating or staying focused on lessons and assignments back then, or is this something that's more of a recent problem that started around the time of the new quarterly planning model at work?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no, it's definitely not just a recent thing with the new planning model. I mean, the planning model just brought it out in a way that affects my job, but if I really think back... I remember this starting way, way before high school. Like, elementary school. \n\nMy mom actually kept all my old report cards in this big plastic bin in the attic, and we were looking through them a couple of years ago when she moved, and it was honestly kind of eye-opening. Because every single teacher, from like second grade all the way up, wrote the exact same things in the comments section. They would always say I was a really bright kid, which, you know, is nice, but then there was always a catch. My elementary school teachers wrote that I was bright but I rushed through my work, or that I talked out of turn constantly. I was always interrupting people or just blurting things out because I felt like if I didn't say it right that second, my thought would just completely vanish. \n\nAnd the homework... oh my gosh, the homework. I would forget it all the time. I'd either forget to write down what the assignment was, or I'd actually do it at home and then just completely forget to turn it in the next morning. My desk was an absolute disaster zone, too. I remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she actually made me stay in during recess once just to clean it out, because it was literally stuffed to the brim with all these crumpled, half-finished papers and old worksheets that I just shoved in there instead of putting them away in my folders. \n\nSo, yeah, it is definitely not new. It is like I have always been this way, just sort of scattered and leaving a trail of unfinished stuff, and I guess I just figured out how to handle it better or hide it better when I got to college and started working, right up until these massive three-hour agency meetings started and I couldn't just skate by on my own schedule anymore...\n\nDoctor: When you were younger and had those thoughts that felt like they would disappear if you didn't say them right away—the ones where you'd blurt things out in class—do you still experience that same feeling now as an adult, where you feel like you have to act on something immediately or the urge will go away?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, actually. I don't think I really do that anymore. I mean, I think I kind of grew out of that specific thing? Or maybe adult life is just set up differently, you know? Like, back in elementary school, you're sitting there at your little desk and the teacher is at the chalkboard, and if you don't raise your hand right that exact second, the moment is completely gone and the class moves on to math or whatever. But now, working at the agency... well, first of all, if I just blurted things out during a pitch with a major client, my VP would probably have a literal heart attack right there in the conference room. So I definitely learned not to do that! \n\nAnd I don't really get that panicked, bubbling-up feeling of, like, 'I have to say this right this second or it's gone forever' anymore. If I have an idea for a campaign, or a question about a media buy while someone is talking, I just... I don't know, I'll jot it down on a sticky note. My desk is literally covered in those little neon pink and yellow Post-its for that exact reason. Or, honestly, I'll just shoot my coworker a Slack message. Slack is like a lifesaver for that, right? Because you can just type the thought the very second it pops into your head without actually interrupting anyone's sentence or derailing the whole meeting. \n\nSo, yeah, I haven't really noticed that being a problem now. I mean, I talk a lot, obviously—my boyfriend always jokes that I could talk to a brick wall and get it to buy ad space—but I don't get that impulsive, desperate urge to just spit words out before they vanish like I did when I was a kid. I feel like I have a pretty good filter now, or at least I just know how to hold onto a thought, or write it down, until it's actually my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your desk is covered in Post-it notes now—how organized do you feel your work is overall? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
With my girlfriends? You know, actually, not so much. I mean, when we get together for drinks or brunch or whatever on the weekends, we all kind of just naturally talk over each other anyway. It's just a big, loud group of women catching up, so it’s hard to tell what’s actually interrupting and what’s just normal conversation for us, you know? 

But... oh, gosh, now that you ask it that way, I do actually do this at work. Like, a lot. It’s not exactly the same panicked feeling as when I was a kid blurting things out in class, but if I’m walking through the office and I suddenly think of an idea... like, say someone else is at their desk working through a pitch deck, or even if a couple of my coworkers are just having a conversation by the printer about a totally different account, I will literally just pop over and interrupt them to add my thought. I just barge right in! 

And it’s so frustrating because, almost the exact second after the words leave my mouth, I realize that it absolutely could have waited. Like, it wasn't an emergency, the client wasn't on fire, they were just trying to format a slide or talk about a media buy, and I just completely derailed their whole train of thought. I don't know why I do it. It's like, if I physically see the deck on their monitor, or I hear a keyword in their conversation, the idea just jumps out of my mouth before my brain can remind me to just go back to my desk and write it on one of my Post-its. My coworkers are usually pretty polite about it, but I always walk away kicking myself because I know I just interrupted their workflow for something that could have easily been an email two hours later. So, yeah, socially it doesn't stand out to me, but in the office I definitely still have that habit of just jumping in when it's really not necessary.

  {

I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Dr. [Name]. Thank you for seeing me. So, honestly, what really made me finally pick up the phone and make this appointment—and I've been debating it for a few months now, probably ever since we shifted to this new quarterly planning model at my agency—is that I keep getting up during our long sales strategy meetings. \n\nI'm an advertising sales agent, and we have these massive, two- or three-hour meetings where we're going over ad buys and client pitches, and I know I’m supposed to just stay seated and take notes like everyone else. But I just... I can't seem to stay in my chair. I'll constantly find reasons to get up. I'll get up just to refill my water, even if my glass is practically full and I really don't need a drink, or I'll wander over and pace by the printer in the back of the conference room, acting like I'm waiting for a slide deck to print out when I'm really not. \n\nAnd the thing is, I know how it looks. The room is mostly glass, everyone else is perfectly planted in their ergonomic chairs, and I'm just popping up and down. My boss even gave me this look last Tuesday when I got up for the third time during the VP's presentation. It's just so frustrating because I know the expectation is to remain seated, but I just end up making excuses to pace around the back of the room anyway, and I feel like it's starting to become a whole thing at work.\n\nDoctor: Outside of these work meetings, do you find yourself having similar difficulties sitting still in other situations—like during meals at home, watching TV, or in social gatherings?\n\nPatient: You know, it's funny you ask that, because I was actually just thinking about this the other day. I don't really have that problem at all outside of work. Like, if I'm at home? I can sit there for literally hours. Just this past Sunday, my boyfriend and I binge-watched almost an entire season of this new true-crime documentary on Netflix, and I was just totally glued to the sofa. I had my blanket, my snacks, and I didn't even want to get up to go to the kitchen. I just sat there the whole afternoon, completely fine. \n\nAnd with meals, it's the same thing. We usually eat on the couch, honestly, but even when we do sit at the actual dining table—like he made this really elaborate baked ziti a couple of nights ago, so we sat at the table to make it a nice dinner—I didn't feel that antsy, crawling-out-of-my-skin feeling at all. We just sat and ate and talked for probably an hour, and I didn't once feel the urge to jump up and check the mail or anything. \n\nEven in social situations, like if I'm out with my girlfriends... we have this spot downtown where we always get a corner booth, and we'll be there for three hours just catching up and having drinks, and I'm perfectly happy just staying put in the booth. I never feel like I need to go pace by the bathrooms or make up an excuse to stand. \n\nSo, no, I really haven't noticed it outside of the office. It's so weird, right? It really just seems to be those specific, long, quiet conference room situations where I'm supposed to be a captive audience and look professional. When it's my own time, or I'm just relaxing or hanging out, I don't have that problem at all. I can just chill. It's just... I don't know, maybe it's the fluorescent lights at the agency or the pressure of the VP being right there, but yeah, at home or out and about, I'm totally fine sitting still.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting in these long meetings and feeling the urge to get up, are you finding it hard to concentrate on what's being said, or is it more that you're feeling anxious or stressed about the meeting situation itself?\n\nPatient: Well, it's definitely the concentration thing. I mean, I don't feel anxious or stressed about the meetings themselves, really. I know my accounts, I know the clients, so it's not like I'm sitting there terrified they're going to call on me and I won't know the answer. It's more like... okay, so we have these really long strategy meetings, right? And they always put up these massive PowerPoint decks. They're just so dense, with all these charts and projections. \n\nI'll sit down, and I'll have my notebook open, and I'll tell myself, 'Okay, this is the Q3 planning meeting, you need to pay attention, this is important.' And I really do start out focused. I'll listen to the first few minutes, maybe jot down a note about a new ad buy or something. But then... I don't know, it's like my brain just sort of unplugs? It happens when I'm reading client briefs at my desk, too. I'll be reading this three-page brief about a new campaign for a local car dealership, and I'll read the first paragraph, and I'm totally with it. But then, suddenly, five or ten minutes have gone by, and I realize I've just been staring at the exact same slide on the projector, or the exact same paragraph on my screen, and I have absolutely no idea what the last point was. \n\nIt's like I'm physically looking at the words, but they aren't going into my brain. The VP will be talking about, I don't know, demographic targeting in the tri-state area, and I'll hear the sound of his voice, but I'm not actually processing a single thing he's saying. And then I have to frantically try to figure out where we are in the deck, or I have to reread the same paragraph in the brief like three or four times just to make it stick. That's usually when I start feeling like I need to get up and walk around, because I'm just sitting there, totally checked out, realizing I've completely lost the thread of the meeting again. It's so frustrating because I really am trying to pay attention, I just... I can't seem to hold onto it for more than a few minutes before I just drift off into space.\n\nDoctor: Going back to when you were in school—elementary, middle, high school—did you have similar difficulties concentrating or staying focused on lessons and assignments back then, or is this something that's more of a recent problem that started around the time of the new quarterly planning model at work?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh, no, it's definitely not just a recent thing with the new planning model. I mean, the planning model just brought it out in a way that affects my job, but if I really think back... I remember this starting way, way before high school. Like, elementary school. \n\nMy mom actually kept all my old report cards in this big plastic bin in the attic, and we were looking through them a couple of years ago when she moved, and it was honestly kind of eye-opening. Because every single teacher, from like second grade all the way up, wrote the exact same things in the comments section. They would always say I was a really bright kid, which, you know, is nice, but then there was always a catch. My elementary school teachers wrote that I was bright but I rushed through my work, or that I talked out of turn constantly. I was always interrupting people or just blurting things out because I felt like if I didn't say it right that second, my thought would just completely vanish. \n\nAnd the homework... oh my gosh, the homework. I would forget it all the time. I'd either forget to write down what the assignment was, or I'd actually do it at home and then just completely forget to turn it in the next morning. My desk was an absolute disaster zone, too. I remember my fourth-grade teacher, Mrs. Gable, she actually made me stay in during recess once just to clean it out, because it was literally stuffed to the brim with all these crumpled, half-finished papers and old worksheets that I just shoved in there instead of putting them away in my folders. \n\nSo, yeah, it is definitely not new. It is like I have always been this way, just sort of scattered and leaving a trail of unfinished stuff, and I guess I just figured out how to handle it better or hide it better when I got to college and started working, right up until these massive three-hour agency meetings started and I couldn't just skate by on my own schedule anymore...\n\nDoctor: When you were younger and had those thoughts that felt like they would disappear if you didn't say them right away—the ones where you'd blurt things out in class—do you still experience that same feeling now as an adult, where you feel like you have to act on something immediately or the urge will go away?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, actually. I don't think I really do that anymore. I mean, I think I kind of grew out of that specific thing? Or maybe adult life is just set up differently, you know? Like, back in elementary school, you're sitting there at your little desk and the teacher is at the chalkboard, and if you don't raise your hand right that exact second, the moment is completely gone and the class moves on to math or whatever. But now, working at the agency... well, first of all, if I just blurted things out during a pitch with a major client, my VP would probably have a literal heart attack right there in the conference room. So I definitely learned not to do that! \n\nAnd I don't really get that panicked, bubbling-up feeling of, like, 'I have to say this right this second or it's gone forever' anymore. If I have an idea for a campaign, or a question about a media buy while someone is talking, I just... I don't know, I'll jot it down on a sticky note. My desk is literally covered in those little neon pink and yellow Post-its for that exact reason. Or, honestly, I'll just shoot my coworker a Slack message. Slack is like a lifesaver for that, right? Because you can just type the thought the very second it pops into your head without actually interrupting anyone's sentence or derailing the whole meeting. \n\nSo, yeah, I haven't really noticed that being a problem now. I mean, I talk a lot, obviously—my boyfriend always jokes that I could talk to a brick wall and get it to buy ad space—but I don't get that impulsive, desperate urge to just spit words out before they vanish like I did when I was a kid. I feel like I have a pretty good filter now, or at least I just know how to hold onto a thought, or write it down, until it's actually my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your desk is covered in Post-it notes now—how organized do you feel your work is overall? Do you find yourself struggling to keep track of deadlines, emails, or projects, or does the Post-it system actually work pretty well for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, the Post-its... well, I mean, they work for getting the thought out of my head in that exact second, like I was saying, but as an actual organizational system? It's... it's kind of a disaster, honestly. I try so hard to be organized, I really do. Every January, I go out and buy one of those really expensive, fancy leather-bound planners—you know the ones, where they have the hourly breakdowns and the little inspirational quotes at the top of every page, and the different colored ribbons to mark your place? I always tell myself, 'Okay, this is the year I'm going to have it all together, everything goes in the planner.' \n\nBut then reality hits, and things get busy. I'll be on a call with a client—like last week, I was talking to the marketing director for this regional grocery chain we represent, and they were changing their whole Q4 strategy—and I'll just frantically scribble a note on a pink sticky note because it's right there. But then later, I'll be on my phone on the train and I'll email myself a reminder about their ad spend. And then I'll get to my desk and open a tab in Chrome with their competitor's pricing, and I just leave the tab open... I have like, I don't know, eighty tabs open across three different browser windows right now. \n\nSo when it actually comes time to sit down and build the campaign strategy or put together the pitch deck, my notes are just completely scattered everywhere. They end up split between my emails, the sticky notes plastered all over my monitor, that expensive planner which I only half-use, and all those random browser tabs. It's just a total mess. And because it's so fragmented, I end up wasting just a ridiculous amount of time trying to piece it all back together. I'll sit there at my desk for forty-five minutes or an hour just staring at everything, trying to figure out what I'm supposed to do first, or which note is the most urgent, instead of just starting the actual work. \n\nIt's like I have to go on a scavenger hunt through my own workspace before I can even begin to do my job. My boyfriend is always telling me I should just use Trello or Asana or one of those project management apps, because that's what his tech company uses, but I feel like if I try to learn a new digital system, I'll just end up with notes in Trello and on the sticky notes and in my email, you know? It wouldn't actually fix the problem, it would just add another place for me to lose my thoughts. So, yeah, I definitely wouldn't say I feel organized overall. It's more like controlled chaos, except half the time I'm not even the one controlling it.\n\nDoctor: When you're out with your girlfriends or in social situations now, do you find yourself interrupting people while they're talking, or do you tend to blurt things out without thinking first?\n\nPatient: With my girlfriends? You know, actually, not so much. I mean, when we get together for drinks or brunch or whatever on the weekends, we all kind of just naturally talk over each other anyway. It's just a big, loud group of women catching up, so it’s hard to tell what’s actually interrupting and what’s just normal conversation for us, you know? \n\nBut... oh, gosh, now that you ask it that way, I do actually do this at work. Like, a lot. It’s not exactly the same panicked feeling as when I was a kid blurting things out in class, but if I’m walking through the office and I suddenly think of an idea... like, say someone else is at their desk working through a pitch deck, or even if a couple of my coworkers are just having a conversation by the printer about a totally different account, I will literally just pop over and interrupt them to add my thought. I just barge right in! \n\nAnd it’s so frustrating because, almost the exact second after the words leave my mouth, I realize that it absolutely could have waited. Like, it wasn't an emergency, the client wasn't on fire, they were just trying to format a slide or talk about a media buy, and I just completely derailed their whole train of thought. I don't know why I do it. It's like, if I physically see the deck on their monitor, or I hear a keyword in their conversation, the idea just jumps out of my mouth before my brain can remind me to just go back to my desk and write it on one of my Post-its. My coworkers are usually pretty polite about it, but I always walk away kicking myself because I know I just interrupted their workflow for something that could have easily been an email two hours later. So, yeah, socially it doesn't stand out to me, but in the office I definitely still have that habit of just jumping in when it's really not necessary.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

  "candidates": ["F90.9"],
  "note": "Ruled out F98.8. Patient shows clear ADHD presentation: lifelong history of inattention (scattered notes, disorganization, difficulty concentrating in meetings), impulsivity (interrupting coworkers, blurting out ideas at work), and hyperactivity (inability to sit still in meetings). However, symptoms are predominantly manifested in work/structured settings rather than across all contexts, which is consistent with adult ADHD where environmental demands reveal previously compensated deficits. The inattention and impulsivity remain present and problematic despite maturation. F98.8 (other specified behavioral/emotional disorders) is less appropriate given the clear ADHD symptom constellation.",
  "is_final": false
}

cut them off.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting my turn on client calls or in office discussions makes me restless, so I keep checking the clock or rehearsing what I want to say until I blurt it out too early.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: If I think of an idea while someone else is working through a deck, I’ll pop over or interrupt their manifestation: I remember this starting way before high school—my elementary school teachers wrote that I was bright but rushed, talked out of turn, forgot homework, and had a desk stuffed with months\n  manifestation: This has been noticeably affecting me since I changed into advertising sales strategy about two years and two months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 30 / advertising_sales_agent / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S011\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: When you were younger and had those thoughts that felt like they would disappear if you didn't say them right away—the ones where you'd blurt things out in class—do you still experience that same feeling now as an adult, where you feel like you have to act on something immediately or the urge will go away?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, actually. I don't think I really do that anymore. I mean, I think I kind of grew out of that specific thing? Or maybe adult life is just set up differently, you know? Like, back in elementary school, you're sitting there at your little desk and the teacher is at the chalkboard, and if you don't raise your hand right that exact second, the moment is completely gone and the class moves on to math or whatever. But now, working at the agency... well, first of all, if I just blurted things out during a pitch with a major client, my VP would probably have a literal heart attack right there in the conference room. So I definitely learned not to do that! \n\nAnd I don't really get that panicked, bubbling-up feeling of, like, 'I have to say this right this second or it's gone forever' anymore. If I have an idea for a campaign, or a question about a media buy while someone is talking, I just... I don't know, I'll jot it down on a sticky note. My desk is literally covered in those little neon pink and yellow Post-its for that exact reason. Or, honestly, I'll just shoot my coworker a Slack message. Slack is like a lifesaver for that, right? Because you can just type the thought the very second it pops into your head without actually interrupting anyone's sentence or derailing the whole meeting. \n\nSo, yeah, I haven't really noticed that being a problem now. I mean, I talk a lot, obviously—my boyfriend always jokes that I could talk to a brick wall and get it to buy ad space—but I don't get that impulsive, desperate urge to just spit words out before they vanish like I did when I was a kid. I feel like I have a pretty good filter now, or at least I just know how to hold onto a thought, or write it down, until it's actually my turn to speak.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your desk is covered in Post-it notes now—how organized do you feel your work is overall? Do you find yourself struggling to keep track of deadlines, emails, or projects, or does the Post-it system actually work pretty well for you?\n\nPatient: Oh, the Post-its... well, I mean, they work for getting the thought out of my head in that exact second, like I was saying, but as an actual organizational system? It's... it's kind of a disaster, honestly. I try so hard to be organized, I really do. Every January, I go out and buy one of those really expensive, fancy leather-bound planners—you know the ones, where they have the hourly breakdowns and the little inspirational quotes at the top of every page, and the different colored ribbons to mark your place? I always tell myself, 'Okay, this is the year I'm going to have it all together, everything goes in the planner.' \n\nBut then reality hits, and things get busy. I'll be on a call with a client—like last week, I was talking to the marketing director for this regional grocery chain we represent, and they were changing their whole Q4 strategy—and I'll just frantically scribble a note on a pink sticky note because it's right there. But then later, I'll be on my phone on the train and I'll email myself a reminder about their ad spend. And then I'll get to my desk and open a tab in Chrome with their competitor's pricing, and I just leave the tab open... I have like, I don't know, eighty tabs open across three different browser windows right now. \n\nSo when it actually comes time to sit down and build the campaign strategy or put together the pitch deck, my notes are just completely scattered everywhere. They end up split between my emails, the sticky notes plastered all over my monitor, that expensive planner which I only half-use, and all those random browser tabs. It's just a total mess. And because it's so fragmented, I end up wasting just a ridiculous amount of time trying to piece it all back together. I'll sit there at my desk for forty-five minutes or an hour just staring at everything, trying to figure out what I'm supposed to do first, or which note is the most urgent, instead of just starting the actual work. \n\nIt's like I have to go on a scavenger hunt through my own workspace before I can even begin to do my job. My boyfriend is always telling me I should just use Trello or Asana or one of those project management apps, because that's what his tech company uses, but I feel like if I try to learn a new digital system, I'll just end up with notes in Trello and on the sticky notes and in my email, you know? It wouldn't actually fix the problem, it would just add another place for me to lose my thoughts. So, yeah, I definitely wouldn't say I feel organized overall. It's more like controlled chaos, except half the time I'm not even the one controlling it.\n\nDoctor: When you're out with your girlfriends or in social situations now, do you find yourself interrupting people while they're talking, or do you tend to blurt things out without thinking first?\n\nPatient: With my girlfriends? It's just a big, loud group of women catching up, so it’s hard to tell what’s actually interrupting and what’s just normal conversation for us, you know? \n\nBut... oh, gosh, now that you ask it that way, I do actually do this at work. Like, a lot. It’s not exactly the same panicked feeling as when I was a kid blurting things out in class, but if I’m walking through the office and I suddenly think of an idea... like, say someone else is at their desk working through a pitch deck, or even if a couple of my coworkers are just having a conversation by the printer about a totally different account, I will literally just pop over and interrupt them to add my thought. I just barge right in! \n\nAnd it’s so frustrating because, almost the exact second after the words leave my mouth, I realize that it absolutely could have waited. Like, it wasn't an emergency, the client wasn't on fire, they were just trying to format a slide or talk about a media buy, and I just completely derailed their whole train of thought. I don't know why I do it. It's like, if I physically see the deck on their monitor, or I hear a keyword in their conversation, the idea just jumps out of my mouth before my brain can remind me to just go back to my desk and write it on one of my Post-its. My coworkers are usually pretty polite about it, but I always walk away kicking myself because I know I just interrupted their workflow for something that could have easily been an email two hours later. So, yeah, socially it doesn't stand out to me, but in the office I definitely still have that habit of just jumping in when it's really not necessary.\n\n[Last Question]\nYou mentioned you interrupt your coworkers at work pretty regularly—do you also find yourself fidgeting, tapping your foot, or feeling restless in situations where you're supposed to be still, beyond just those long conference room meetings? Oh, um... fidgeting? No, actually, not really. I mean, I know some people are constant leg-bouncers or pen-clickers—like the guy in the cubicle next to me, Dave, he clicks his pen literally all day long and it drives me absolutely insane when I'm trying to concentrate—but I don't really do anything like that.
